Yeah, um… I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is I keep getting up when I really should be staying seated. Like during sprint planning at work, or even on calls with the mortgage people, I’ll suddenly decide I need water or I should check something in the other room.

Yeah, it definitely goes back. I mean, I didn’t think of it as a “thing” then, but in elementary school I remember teachers moving my desk around or putting me closer to them because I was always popping up out of my seat.

I guess it happened when I was younger too, but now I notice it more because the stuff I’m supposed to focus on actually matters.\n\n[Last Question]\nBeyond work, do these patterns of restlessness or losing focus also cause problems for you at home or in your personal relationships?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah, it does. I mean, not in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it’s starting to be… noticeable, I guess.

At home, my partner has to circle back with me on things more than they used to — like plans or little tasks we said we’d handle. And then at work it’s similar, where someone follows up and I’m like, “Right, yes, I meant to do that,” and I hate that feeling. It makes me worry I’m becoming the unreliable one, especially with planning stuff.

Which is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing things off. I’ll have every intention of doing the thing, and then somehow it turns into this whole chain of reminders and follow-ups. And then I feel bad because I know everyone’s busy — sorry, I’m rambling — but yes, it’s causing friction both at work and at home.

I don’t know… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed anything like that as the main thing. I get stressed, sure, but who doesn’t? Especially with work deadlines and all the random life admin stuff — I swear there’s always some form or email or something I forgot to answer.

I think it’s been there pretty much the whole time, yeah. I mean, it probably looked different depending on the age, but in elementary school I remember teachers moving my desk around or putting me closer to them because I couldn’t stay put.

Sprint planning is already kind of a whole thing anyway—we use this board that everyone has opinions about—but, yeah, the main thing is I can’t seem to stay put when I’m supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did you notice this kind of restlessness and difficulty staying seated as a child or teenager, or is this something that's really only become a problem more recently, say in the last several months or years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely goes back. I mean, I didn’t think of it as a “thing” then, but in elementary school I remember teachers moving my desk around or putting me closer to them because I was always popping up out of my seat.\n\nAnd I’d talk before I was called on, too—like I knew the answer and it would just come out. Or I’d rush through assignments even when I understood them, which is funny because I actually liked school for the most part. I had this one teacher who used to tap my desk with her pencil—sorry, that’s probably not important—but, yeah, it wasn’t just recently. It was there when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, do you also notice trouble staying focused on tasks, following through on details, or often losing track of what you're doing—both now and back when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes, there is some of that. It’s not always dramatic, but I’ll sit down to review a pull request and I really do mean to focus on it, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code and my brain is off thinking about, like, whether I paid the water bill or what we’re doing for dinner.\n\nSame thing with the mortgage documents recently — which, honestly, those are kind of impossible to read anyway — but I’ll start with good intentions and then catch myself rereading the same section over and over because I’ve drifted off into five unrelated thoughts. I guess it happened when I was younger too, but now I notice it more because the stuff I’m supposed to focus on actually matters.\n\nDoctor: Beyond work, do these patterns of restlessness or losing focus also cause problems for you at home or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, not in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it’s starting to be… noticeable, I guess.\n\nAt home, my partner has to circle back with me on things more than they used to — like plans or little tasks we said we’d handle. And then at work it’s similar, where someone follows up and I’m like, “Right, yes, I meant to do that,” and I hate that feeling. It makes me worry I’m becoming the unreliable one, especially with planning stuff.\n\nWhich is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing things off. I’ll have every intention of doing the thing, and then somehow it turns into this whole chain of reminders and follow-ups. And then I feel bad because I know everyone’s busy — sorry, I’m rambling — but yes, it’s causing friction both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any ongoing feelings of anxiety, worry, or low mood recently that might be contributing to these concentration and restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed anything like that as the main thing. I get stressed, sure, but who doesn’t? Especially with work deadlines and all the random life admin stuff — I swear there’s always some form or email or something I forgot to answer.\n\nBut low mood or ongoing anxiety… I’m not sure. It doesn’t feel like that’s what’s driving it, at least not to me. I can be in a perfectly fine mood and still have the same issue where I’m bouncing around mentally or can’t stay with one thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these attention and restlessness difficulties have been fairly constant throughout your life from childhood into adulthood, or were there stretches—like in high school or early adulthood—where they were much less noticeable or seemed to go away?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much the whole time, yeah. I mean, it probably looked different depending on the age, but in elementary school I remember teachers moving my desk around or putting me closer to them because I couldn’t stay put.\n\nLike I’d pop up out of my seat, or start talking before they called on me, or I’d rush through assignments even when I actually understood the work. Which is funny because I liked school in some ways — I liked getting new notebooks, and I was weirdly particular about pencils — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, I don’t remember it fully going away. And then I feel bad because I know everyone’s busy — sorry, I’m rambling — but yes, it’s causing friction both at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any ongoing feelings of anxiety, worry, or low mood recently that might be contributing to these concentration and restlessness issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed anything like that as the main thing. I get stressed, sure, but who doesn’t? Especially with work deadlines and all the random life admin stuff — I swear there’s always some form or email or something I forgot to answer.\n\nBut low mood or ongoing anxiety… I’m not sure. It doesn’t feel like that’s what’s driving it, at least not to me. I can be in a perfectly fine mood and still have the same issue where I’m bouncing around mentally or can’t stay with one thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these attention and restlessness difficulties have been fairly constant throughout your life from childhood into adulthood, or were there stretches—like in high school or early adulthood—where they were much less noticeable or seemed to go away?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much the whole time, yeah. I mean, it probably looked different depending on the age, but in elementary school I remember teachers moving my desk around or putting me closer to them because I couldn’t stay put.\n\nLike I’d pop up out of my seat, or start talking before they called on me, or I’d rush through assignments even when I actually understood the work. Which is funny because I liked school in some ways — I liked getting new notebooks, and I was weirdly particular about pencils — sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, I don’t remember it fully going away. It was more like sometimes I could compensate better, or the situation made it less obvious.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had distinct periods—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than normal, or felt on top of the world, followed by a return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so? I usually still need sleep, and if I don’t get it, I feel it. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S011\",\"name\":\"Leaving_Seat\",\"description\":\"Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\",\"reason\":\"This is the first reply of the consultation, so it should return the chief complaint symptom code.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. It makes me worry I’m becoming the unreliable one, especially with planning stuff.\n\nWhich is frustrating because I really am trying. I’m not just blowing things off. And then I feel bad because I know everyone’s busy — sorry, I’m rambling — but yes, it’s causing friction both at work and at home.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t know… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed anything like that as the main thing.   }

Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger.
